    (ii); and writing $M(u) = \prod_{x \in u}\mathrm{mult}[x] \ge 1$,
    $\Phi(uv) = M(v)\,\Phi(u) + (M(v){-}1)\,\#c(u) + \Phi(v) \ge
    \Phi(u)$, so any string containing $B$ has $\Phi \ge 1$.  The orbit
    can therefore neither stop while $B$ occurs -- a sweep finding an
    occurrence fires at the leftmost one -- nor fire more than
    $\Phi(s_0)/\delta$ times, and when $B$ is finally absent the orbit is
    at a fixed point (Lemma~\ref{lem:fpsweep}).
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{thm:affine} does not end the story, in either direction.
Counting potentials extend it -- $\Phi = \#g_1 + w\,\#g_2$ over
$k$-grams of $B$, e.g.\ the overlapping count of $aa$ for
$[\,aba/aa\,]$, giving $K \le n^{k}$ -- and cover further convergent
rules. And the affine method has genuine limits: the rule
$[\,aaab/baa\,]$ converges but admits no affine potential -- the map
equality (i) forces $\mathrm{mult}[a] = 1$ and then $\mathrm{off}[a] =
0$, killing the only letter with $\delta \ge 1$. On every rule a
potential reaches, the single-exponential law follows; beyond them the
conjecture stands.

\subsection{Both Directions at Once}
\label{ssec:union}

The variants so far reinterpret the node; the \emph{union} $L{+}\mathrm{R}$ extends the grammar, admitting both node kinds $[\,R/P\,]$ and $[\,R/P\,]^{\mathrm{R}}$ in one expression. It is the weakest calculus that edits from both ends in a single pipeline, and its placement reduces to one function. Write
\begin{align*}
    \mathrm{r}(A, B, C) \;=\; [\,A/B\,]^{\mathrm{R}} C
\end{align*}
for the right pass as a three-argument function -- the denotation of the one-node expression $[\,X_1/X_2\,]^{\mathrm{R}} X_3$, undefined exactly when $B = \epsilon$ -- and $\bar{\mathrm{r}}$ for its mirror $m(\mathrm{r})$, the left pass as a three-argument function.

\begin{theorem}
    (\emph{Collapse Criterion})
    \label{thm:union-collapse}
    The following are equivalent: $L{+}\mathrm{R} = L$; $\mathrm{R} \sqsubseteq L$; $\mathrm{r} \in L$. Each is equivalent to $L{+}\mathrm{R} = \mathrm{R}$ and to $\bar{\mathrm{r}} \in \mathrm{R}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    If $\llbracket E_{\mathrm{r}} \rrbracket = \mathrm{r}$, translate a mixed expression bottom-up, replacing every $\mathrm{R}$-node $[\,R/P\,]^{\mathrm{R}} E$ with $E_{\mathrm{r}}[\,t(R)/X_1,\ t(P)/X_2,\ t(E)/X_3\,]$: by Lemma~\ref{lem:beta} and induction the translation denotes the same partial function. On defined values this is the node's semantics. On undefinedness: $\mathrm{r}$ depends on each of its three arguments (fix two, vary the third), an expression cannot depend on a variable it does not contain, so all three variables occur in $E_{\mathrm{r}}$ and Lemma~\ref{lem:beta}'s clause applies; and $\mathrm{r}$ is undefined exactly at $B = \epsilon$, which is the node's condition too. Conversely $[\,X_1/X_2\,]^{\mathrm{R}} X_3$ is itself a mixed expression, giving the reverse implication and the restriction to pure-$\mathrm{R}$ expressions. The final clause is the mirror $m$ of Corollary~\ref{cor:incl-symmetry}: $m$ preserves the union's reachable class, carries the $L$-class onto the $\mathrm{R}$-class, and $m(\bar{\mathrm{r}}) = \mathrm{r}$.
\end{proof}

So the union collapses onto $L$ exactly when the right pass, as a function of all three of its arguments, is itself an $L$-expression -- and reversal's reachability (Theorem~\ref{thm:conjugation}) is sufficient for this but, by the criterion, not necessary. (Reversal is now proved unreachable -- Section~\ref{ssec:frontier} -- so the sufficient route is closed and the criterion stands alone.)

\begin{lemma}
    (\emph{Disjoint Commutation})
    \label{lem:disjoint-comm}
    Passes $[\,A/B\,]^{d_1}$ and $[\,C/D\,]^{d_2}$ -- either direction pairing -- commute whenever $\mathrm{alph}(B) \cap \mathrm{alph}(D) = \emptyset$, $A \neq \epsilon$ with $\mathrm{alph}(A) \cap \mathrm{alph}(D) = \emptyset$, and $C \neq \epsilon$ with $\mathrm{alph}(C) \cap \mathrm{alph}(B) = \emptyset$. Every hypothesis is necessary.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    A $B$-match and a $D$-match sharing a position would place a letter of $\mathrm{alph}(B) \cap \mathrm{alph}(D)$ there; the hypotheses exclude this, so the two patterns' matches in any string are pairwise disjoint windows. Replacing a $B$-window by $A$ creates no $D$-match -- a straddling match would need a $D$-letter at an inserted position, and $A$ carries none -- and destroys none, since no $D$-window met a $B$-window; symmetrically for the second pass. Both compositions therefore compute the same simultaneous replacement. For necessity: $[\,\epsilon/c\,]$ before $[\,d/ab\,]$ on $\mathtt{acb}$ yields $\mathtt{ab}$, the reverse order $\mathtt{d}$ -- deletion splices fresh matches, which is why the replacements must be nonempty -- and the disjointness clauses admit two-letter counterexamples of the same size.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    (\emph{No Two-Block Normal Form})
    \label{prop:no-2block}
    Over the four cross-letter halvings $\{[\,a/bb\,], [\,b/aa\,]\} \times \{L, \mathrm{R}\}$: the four sibling pipelines $[\,a/bb\,] \cdot [\,b/aa\,]^{\mathrm{R}} \cdot [\,a/bb\,]$, its mirror, and their two renamings are equivalent to no two-block composite $L^j \circ \mathrm{R}^k$ or $\mathrm{R}^k \circ L^j$ \emph{at any depth} -- and of the $64$ mixed words of three passes, exactly these four are not two-block composites. The siblings fix every alternating string, both patterns being absent from it.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    A closure computation on the function tables over the $255$ binary strings of length $\leq 7$. The pure-$L$ and pure-$\mathrm{R}$ fragments saturate at $15$ functions each -- closed under composition, so the computation covers composites of \emph{every} depth, not a bounded budget. The two-block composites $L^j \circ \mathrm{R}^k$ and $\mathrm{R}^k \circ L^j$ close at $69$ functions, among which the siblings' functions do not appear: the first claim. For the second, the same enumeration restricted to the $64$ mixed words of three passes leaves exactly the four siblings outside the two-block class.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    (\emph{Orientation})
    \label{lem:orientation}
    On a two-letter alphabet, the halving passes lay their output down in opposite orders: on a maximal run of length $n$, bare or sentinel-delimited, $[\,a/bb\,]$ produces the block $a^{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor}$ \emph{before} the residue $b^{\,n \bmod 2}$, and $[\,a/bb\,]^{\mathrm{R}}$ the residue \emph{before} the block.
\end{lemma}

The growth theory transfers unchanged: an $\mathrm{R}$-pass obeys the length bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:length} (its matches are disjoint either way), so the degree recursion and Theorem~\ref{thm:fp} carry over verbatim, and every $L{+}\mathrm{R}$-reachable function is polynomial-time with degree bounded by its expression. Concatenation is eliminable in the union as in $L$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:core}, whose induction is direction-agnostic), and by Lemma~\ref{lem:disjoint-comm} a mixed pipeline whose patterns are pairwise alphabet-disjoint and met only by nonempty disjoint replacements is equivalent to a pure-$L$ one -- interleaving, in other words, \emph{requires} overlapping, hence bordered, patterns (Proposition~\ref{prop:r2l-agree}).

\begin{conjecture}
    (\emph{Strictness of the Union})
    \label{conj:union}
    $L{+}\mathrm{R} \supsetneq L$ -- equivalently $\mathrm{r} \notin L$, $\bar{\mathrm{r}} \notin \mathrm{R}$, $L \neq \mathrm{R}$. In particular it would follow that reversal is not $L$-reachable.
\end{conjecture}

The case is threefold. (i) The constant fragments are provably incomparable (Theorem~\ref{thm:subsequential}). (ii) The obstruction has a mechanism (Lemma~\ref{lem:orientation}): an $\mathrm{R}$-halving leaves its residue at the run \emph{front} while every $L$ mechanism computes per-run residues only at run \emph{ends}, and moving a marker from where it is computed to where it is needed is right-to-left information flow, which an $L$-pass's frontier cannot perform; three concrete constructions -- pad-then-pair, comma-code parity marking, offset-$1$ pairing -- each fail with their own counterexample. (iii) The boundary of the easy invariants is located: five output-shaped formalizations of right-to-left routing are each falsified by plain $L$-constructions, and the strongest -- a first-difference offset -- separates the identity and the toolkit from $[\,a/bb\,]^{\mathrm{R}}$ and the sibling $W$ of Proposition~\ref{prop:no-2block}, falling only to deletion under an \emph{input-computed} pattern, the $\mathtt{rep}$ escape hatch of Theorem~\ref{lem:multiple substitution}. A proof of Conjecture~\ref{conj:union}, if one exists, must be dependence-structural rather than output-shaped; anchored junction patterns -- the shape of Proposition~\ref{prop:last} -- are the natural first place to look for a collapse witness. Reversal itself is now proved unreachable in $L$ (Section~\ref{ssec:frontier}), which settles the conjecture's ``in particular'' independently of the conjecture; the exact criterion $\mathrm{r} \in L$ is not decided by it and remains open.

\subsection{The Landscape}

\begin{center}
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l|llll}
    variant & $L \sqsubseteq V$ & $V \sqsubseteq L$ & toolkit & growth \\
    \hline
    once & refuted & open & rebuilt & linear \\
    positional & refuted & iff once $\in L$ & $\mathtt{tail}$, $\mathtt{head}$ & linear \\
    $\mathrm{lim}$ & trivially & refuted & verbatim & unbounded \\
    right-to-left & open & open & verbatim & polynomial \\
    union ($L{+}\mathrm{R}$) & trivially & conj.\ no & verbatim & polynomial
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The right-to-left row's two ``open'' cells and the union row's one are a single question: either inclusion implies the other (Corollary~\ref{cor:incl-symmetry}), and $L{+}\mathrm{R} \sqsubseteq L$ iff $\mathrm{R} \sqsubseteq L$ iff $\mathrm{r} \in L$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:union-collapse}) -- Conjecture~\ref{conj:union} predicts the negative answer for all three. Both inclusions hold if $\mathrm{rev}$ is reachable in either calculus, and no other route between $L$ and $\mathrm{R}$ is known (Theorem~\ref{thm:subsequential} separates only the constant fragments). The two ``refuted'' entries of the once and positional rows are Corollaries~\ref{cor:once-sep} and~\ref{cor:pos-sep}. The $\mathrm{lim}$ row is the only one whose class is exactly characterized (Corollary~\ref{cor:limclass}) -- its ``refuted'' is Proposition~\ref{prop:limgrowth}, one constant pass under one iteration node already outgrowing Lemma~\ref{lem:length} -- and the only one with unbounded growth.

The web hangs on two ropes, both reachability questions about the baseline itself; the first is open, the second is settled:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \emph{Is the once-primitive $L$-reachable?} By Theorem~\ref{thm:pos-hinge} this decides $\mathrm{POS} \sqsubseteq L$, and with it the whole once-family's relation to $L$. The probe is affirmative over two letters (Proposition~\ref{prop:del-leftmost}), and the marking reduction collapses the rest: over every alphabet, constant needle or computed, the question is the single binary node $[\,\mathtt{babba}/\mathtt{baa}\,]_1$ on the marked texts, where the cell-prefix function would already decide it (Theorem~\ref{thm:marking}, Corollary~\ref{cor:once-crux}, Proposition~\ref{prop:cellprefix}). The hinge itself is one question, not one per alphabet: the once node as a three-argument function is coding-equivariant (Corollary~\ref{cor:uniform}), so an answer over any one alphabet of at least two letters is the answer over all -- the alphabet-sensitivity above is about native conjugates, not the hinge.
    \item \emph{Is reversal $L$-reachable?} This decides whether $L = \mathrm{R}$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:conjugation}), and by the mirror symmetry of the once-calculi it is also the hinge between once-left and once-right. (Section~\ref{sec:recursion} settles the recursive version: reversal is computable there by a three-line guarded recursion; and the bounded calculus can already read and edit its right end, Proposition~\ref{prop:last} -- the question is now sharp: the bounded calculus, or nothing.) The union calculus (Section~\ref{ssec:union}) refines the stakes: $\mathrm{rev} \in L$ is sufficient but not necessary for $L = \mathrm{R}$ -- the exact criterion is $\mathrm{r} \in L$, which we conjecture is not the case (Conjecture~\ref{conj:union}). Section~\ref{sec:alphabet} makes the question alphabet-uniform: $\mathrm{rev} \in L$ over one alphabet of at least two letters iff over every (Theorem~\ref{thm:rev-uniform}) -- as with the once hinge, a single negative answer, proved over any one alphabet, would settle all. Section~\ref{ssec:frontier} maps the terrain and closes it: negative over unbounded alphabets at every depth, positive on every fixed separator structure -- any number of separators, repeats included, over any finite alphabet -- so that no fixed-alphabet obstruction can live anywhere at fixed structure; and negative at varying separator count: no such expression exists, the failure effective at separator counts linear in the pass count (the dichotomy, Section~\ref{ssec:frontier}) -- so this problem's answer is negative, reversal is not $L$-reachable over any finite alphabet of at least two letters, and by the uniformity all of them at once.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{remark}
    (\emph{The Price of Reordering})
    \label{rem:price}
    The second hinge -- is reversal $L$-reachable? (answered negatively, Section~\ref{ssec:frontier}) -- has a quantitative form. For a function $f$ and an input $w$, let $w^{p}$ be $w$ with its $p$-th character flipped, and let
    \[
        D_f(w,p) \;\triangleq\; \{\, i : f(w)_i \neq f(w^{p})_i \,\},
    \]
    discarding the flips that change the output length. Where the surviving rows are singletons meeting distinct columns, they induce a partial injective map $\pi_w$ from input positions to output positions, and
    \[
        \chi_f(w) \;\triangleq\; \#\{(p < p') : \pi_w(p) > \pi_w(p')\}
    \]
    counts its \emph{crossings}: the disorder of $f$'s output routing. Reading the right end is free -- $\mathtt{last}$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:last}) routes its single far cell with zero crossings -- and so is dropping the final character ($\mathtt{init}$, the same proposition). What costs crossings is \emph{moving} characters, and the costs of the functions the calculus demonstrably \emph{can} build form a ladder (all rows at $|w| = 14$, worst input; the $k$-th rung family $\mathrm{revlast}_k(w) \triangleq w_{n-1} w_{n-2} \cdots w_{n-k} \cdot w[:n{-}k]$ is $L$-expressible for every fixed $k$, by $k$ anchored right-end reads $\mathtt{last} \circ \mathtt{init}^{j}$ ($j < k$) and the remaining head $\mathtt{init}^{k}$, glued with $\mathtt{cat}$):

    \begin{center}
        \small
        \begin{tabular}{llll}
            \hline
            $f$ & in $L$? & $\chi_f$ & closed form \\
            \hline
            $\mathtt{init}$ & yes & $0$ & order-preserving \\
            $\mathtt{last}$ & yes & $0$ & reads the far end \\
            $\mathrm{rotR} = \mathtt{cat}(\mathtt{last},\mathtt{init})$ & yes & $13$ & $n-1$ \\
            swap-first-last & yes & $25$ & $2(n{-}2)+1$ \\
            $\mathrm{revlast}_k$, $k=1,2,3,4$ & yes, fixed $k$ & $13,\,25,\,36,\,46$ & $\binom{n}{2}-\binom{n-k}{2}$ \\
            $\mathrm{rev}$ & \emph{open} & $91$ & $\binom{n}{2}$ \\
            \hline
        \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    The ladder is exact: $\mathrm{revlast}_k$ reverses a $k$-character tail block and moves it to the front, so its crossings are the block's internal reversals $\binom{k}{2}$ plus its crossings of the untouched head $k(n-k)$ -- which sum to $\binom{n}{2} - \binom{n-k}{2}$, the number of pairs involving at least one moved character. Each fixed $k$ is affordable; reversal is the $k = n$ rung, and it is the only row whose crossing count is quadratic in $n$. Every rung of the ladder pays $O(n)$ crossings, which suggests a quantitative form of the negative answer: a constant $c(E)$, depending only on the expression, with $\chi_{\llbracket E \rrbracket}(w) \leq c(E)\cdot|w|$ for all $w$; reversal needs $|w|(|w|{-}1)/2$.

    Three delimitations keep the suggestion honest. \emph{(i) Pair counts are the wrong measure.} The doubling $\mathtt{cat}(X,X)$ repeats the input, and under the natural correspondence sending each input position to its two output copies, $\binom{n}{2}$ pairs come out inverted -- for input positions $i < j$, the first copy of $j$ lands before the second copy of $i$. The doubling is as cheap as an expression gets, so any obstruction that counts inverted pairs of a natural input--output correspondence is falsified on the spot; the crossing count survives it because it is computed flip by flip, and the doubling's flips each move the two copies of one character together -- non-singleton rows, discarded by construction. \emph{(ii) Content is relabelable.} Label each character of $\mathrm{rev}(w)$ by the input position it comes from, consistently (same character, distinct positions); there is such a labeling increasing within each character class -- each class of $\mathrm{rev}(w)$ is a class of $w$ -- and a strictly decreasing subsequence of it uses at most one position per class, at most $|\Sigma|$ in all. So no invariant computable from output content alone can distinguish $\mathrm{rev}$ from an order-preserving function; a proof must constrain which input characters the calculus delivers where. \emph{(iii) Tracing is fakeable.} The anchored constructions of the ladder rebuild their outputs from constant characters -- Proposition~\ref{prop:last} \emph{emits} the right end rather than moving it -- so an invariant that demands output characters be moved input characters excludes none of the anchored rungs and constrains a hypothetical reversal witness only on its verbatim routes. The crossing count is defined through output influence, which constant reconstruction cannot fake; that is the sense in which it carries the suggestion above.

    Finally, the obstruction the ladder measures is \emph{reversal-specific}. The canonical functions of the two sibling questions -- the longest $b$-free prefix (truncation at the leftmost $b$, the left end of the once-problem's probe, ``delete the leftmost $b$'') and the right-to-left pass $[\,b/aa\,]^{\mathrm{R}}$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:subsequential}'s separating witness) -- have $\chi = 0$: a prefix cut and a local halving reorder nothing globally -- every flip either leaves their output unchanged or changes its length, and the count discards the length-changing flips. A crossing-count proof that $\mathrm{rev} \notin L$ therefore cannot double for either sibling; unbounded reordering, the computed needle, and residue routing are three obstructions, and the ladder prices only the first.
\end{remark}

Beyond the two hinges:
\begin{enumerate} \setcounter{enumi}{2}
    \item Is convergence of $\mathrm{lim}([\,A/B\,])$ decidable? Proposition~\ref{prop:limtotal} settles everything outside the case $|A| > |B|$ with $B \not\subset A$; the affine potentials of Theorem~\ref{thm:affine} are a decidable sufficient condition there. The unrestricted form is the long-standing open one-rule termination problem \cite{geser01}, whose known decidable classes -- left-hand sides of the shape $a^*b^*$ \cite{senizergues96}, inhibitors \cite{mcnaughton01}, grid rules \cite{geser02}, one-overlap-pair \cite{geser03}, single-threaded derivations \cite{moczydlowski05} -- decide a different question.
    \item Is there a growth dichotomy for the one-rule $\mathrm{lim}$ fragment? One node over one constant pass reaches exponential output and Fibonacci divergent orbits (Proposition~\ref{prop:limgrowth}, Remark~\ref{rem:fiborbit}), and the single-exponential law conjectures nothing beyond. Growth of one-rule systems is the classical subject of derivational complexity \cite{kobayashi01}.
    \item The exact characterization of the reachable class for each variant -- open problem 1 of Section~\ref{sec:limits}, now one question per row.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Alphabet Invariance}
\label{sec:alphabet}

Every construction so far has fixed its alphabet, and two letters always sufficed. This section addresses the fourth open problem of Section~\ref{sec:limits} head on: \emph{which functions are reachable over every alphabet with $|\Sigma| \geq 2$, and does a larger alphabet ever add power?} Write $f \in L_\Sigma$ for: $f$ is reachable by an expression over $\Sigma$ -- the landscape's notation. Up to a canonical conjugation, the class does not depend on the alphabet at all: for any two alphabets of at least two letters and any \emph{good coding} between them, $f \in L_\Sigma$ iff its conjugate $f^c \in L_\Gamma$. The boundary of the invariance is exactly $|\Sigma| = 1$: the conjugation exists one way and cannot exist the other, for a structural reason. As corollaries, the two hinges of the landscape are each a single question rather than one per alphabet: the once node and the right pass are coding-equivariant, and reversal over one alphabet of at least two letters is reachable iff over every. The reversal hinge has since been mapped at both ends of the alphabet question and closed: impossible over unbounded alphabets at any depth, computable on every fixed separator structure over any finite alphabet -- the separator letters may repeat, so the same-letter families are included -- and impossible at varying separator count: no single expression computes reversal on all of $\Sigma^*$, the failure effective at separator counts linear in the pass count (Section~\ref{ssec:frontier}).

\subsection{Comma-Free Codings}

\begin{definition}
    (\emph{Good Codings})
    \label{def:code}
    A \emph{coding} is an injective monoid homomorphism $c : \Sigma^* \rightarrow \Gamma^*$ with $D_c \triangleq c(\Sigma)$ of uniform length $\ell \geq 1$ (a \emph{dictionary}); write $c(S)$ for the letter-by-letter extension. The coding is \emph{good} if $D_c$ is \emph{comma-free} (Golomb's condition \cite{golomb58}; the theory of codes treats it under synchronizing codes \cite{berstel85,lothaire97}): no $w \in D_c$ occurs in $u \cdot v$ at any of the junction-straddling positions $1, \dots, \ell-1$ ($u, v \in D_c$).
\end{definition}

Comma-freeness is the Golomb analogue of the Comma Code Lemma for $\mathtt{enc}^2_{b,x}$ (blocks $xc$): it is exactly the self-synchronization that makes a coding usable inside a scanning calculus. Without it the conjugacy class of a function depends on the coding, and the notion degenerates: a free assignment with $\ell(SS) = \ell(S)^2$, say, makes $X \mapsto XX$ conjugate to the unary squaring map. The good codings are the ones on which the conjugation is canonical.

\begin{lemma}
    (\emph{Existence: the Double-$a$ Family})
    \label{lem:dict}
    Let $\Gamma$ have two distinct letters $a \ne b$ and $\ell \geq 3$. Then
    \begin{align}
        D_\ell \;\triangleq\; \{\, a\,a\,m\,b \;:\; m \in \Gamma^{\ell-3},\ aa \not\le m,\ m[0] \ne a \,\}
    \end{align}
    is comma-free, of uniform length $\ell$; over the binary alphabet $|D_\ell| = \mathrm{Fib}(\ell-2)$ (Fibonacci numbers, $\mathrm{Fib}(1) = \mathrm{Fib}(2) = 1$). So good codings exist between any two alphabets with at least two letters, in both directions.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Every codeword starts with $aa$, contains $aa$ only at position $0$ (the interior $m$ avoids $aa$ and, for $\ell > 3$, does not start with $a$), and ends with $b$. In $u \cdot v$ the factor $aa$ therefore occurs only at $0$ and $\ell$ (the junction reads $b \cdot a$), and any occurrence of a codeword, starting as it does with $aa$, starts at $0$ or $\ell$ -- aligned. The count: $m$ is $b$ followed by an $aa$-free word of length $\ell-4$, counted by $\mathrm{Fib}(\ell-2)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    (\emph{Self-Synchronization})
    \label{lem:selfsync}
    Let $c$ be good and $B \ne \epsilon$. The occurrences of $c(B)$ in $c(C)$ are exactly $\{\, \ell \cdot s \;:\; s$ an occurrence of $B$ in $C \,\}$; in particular every occurrence of a codeword inside a codeword-sequence starts at a multiple of $\ell$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Uniform lengths first: an occurrence of $w \in D_c$ (length $\ell$) lying wholly inside a single codeword (length $\ell$) of the surrounding sequence starts at offset $0$. Otherwise the occurrence straddles at least one junction $j\ell$ with $p < j\ell < p + \ell$, and its restrictions to the two flanking codewords exhibit $w$ in their product at the junction-straddling position $p - (j{-}1)\ell \in [1, \ell-1]$ -- against comma-freeness. For the block $c(B)$: its first codeword $c(B[0]) \in D_c$ occurs at a multiple of $\ell$ by the first part, the block then covers whole codewords of $c(C)$, and these spell $B$; the converse is immediate.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    (\emph{Pass Transfer})
    \label{lem:pass-transfer}
    Let $c$ be good. For all $A, C \in \Sigma^*$ and $B \in \Sigma^* \setminus \{\epsilon\}$,
    \begin{align}
        [\,c(A)/c(B)\,]\; c(C) \;=\; c\big([\,A/B\,]C\big),
    \end{align}
    and the same identity holds for the once and rightmost primitives (Definitions~\ref{def:once} and~\ref{def:r2l}): the greedy occurrence lists correspond order-isomorphically under $s \mapsto \ell s$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Induction along the scan. By Self-Synchronization the occurrence lists correspond, and the correspondence preserves order and disjointness (positions scale by $\ell$), so leftmost and rightmost both transfer; it is direction-free, so the right-to-left variant transfers too. For the full pass, write $C = X B Y$ with $|X|$ minimal: the first occurrence of $c(B)$ in $c(X)\,c(B)\,c(Y)$ is the aligned one at $\ell|X|$ (any earlier one would be an aligned occurrence inside $c(X)$, i.e.\ an occurrence of $B$ in $X$), the scan resumes at $\ell(|X|{+}|B|)$ -- aligned again, at the start of $c(Y)$ -- and the inserted $c(A)$ is never re-entered, matching the never-rescan clause of Definition~\ref{def:subst}. Both sides now split as $c([A/B]X) \cdot c(A) \cdot c([A/B]Y)$ with $|X|, |Y| < |C|$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Transfer Theorem}

\begin{definition}
    (\emph{Conjugate})
    \label{def:conjugate}
    For $f$ an $n$-ary partial function on $\Sigma^*$ and $c : \Sigma \rightarrow \Gamma^\ell$ a good coding, the \emph{conjugate} $f^c : c(\Sigma^*)^n \rightharpoonup c(\Sigma^*)$ is $c \circ f \circ c^{-1}$ -- a partial function in the sense of the restriction clause of Definition~\ref{def:reachable}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    (\emph{Direction 1})
    \label{thm:transfer-forward}
    Let $E \in \mathrm{Exp}_n$ over $\Sigma$ and $c$ good. Define $E^c$ by: constants $W \mapsto c(W)$, variables and both constructors unchanged. Then
    \begin{align}
        \llbracket E^c \rrbracket\big(c(\vec S)\big) \;=\; c\big(\llbracket E \rrbracket(\vec S)\big)
    \end{align}
    whenever the right-hand side is defined, and the two sides are undefined together (the pattern is $\epsilon$ on one side iff on the other). Moreover $|E^c| = |E|$ (node count; constant symbols grow $\ell$-fold in word length), $\deg(E^c) = \deg(E)$, and $\mathrm{Safe}(E) \Rightarrow \mathrm{Safe}(E^c)$. Finally, if $\llbracket E \rrbracket$ is total, a \emph{total} $\Gamma$-expression agreeing with $E^c$ on $c(\Sigma^*)^n$ exists: replace, simultaneously, every pattern sub-expression $P^c$ of $E^c$ by $\gamma(P^c)$, where $\gamma(X) \triangleq \mathtt{if}(\mathtt{eq}(X, \epsilon), b, X)$ for a fixed letter $b \in \Gamma$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Induction on $E$. Variables and constants are immediate; concatenation commutes with $c$ because $c$ is a homomorphism. For the pass node the patterns and replacements are the $c$-images of the sub-values, and Pass Transfer applies at every value of the sub-expressions; the definedness clause is the $\epsilon$-condition, and $c(B) = \epsilon$ iff $B = \epsilon$. Safety and degree are structural ($c(W)$ is a nonempty constant iff $W$ is, so $\mathrm{Anch}$ transfers; $\deg$ sums over the scrutinee and replacement of a pass, and each slot's transfer preserves it by induction, constants having degree $0$ on both sides). For the total extension: on inputs from $c(\Sigma^*)^n$ every sub-value of $E^c$ is itself a $c$-image (apply the equation to each sub-expression), and every pattern sub-value is nonempty -- a total $\llbracket E \rrbracket$ forces nonempty pattern values on \emph{every} input, by strictness -- so $\gamma$ acts as the identity exactly on those, and the wrapped expression computes $E^c$'s values on the image; and a $\gamma$-output is never empty while every other node type is total, so the wrapped expression is total everywhere. The guard sits on the pattern, as in the total variant of $\mathrm{rep}_n$ (Section~\ref{sec:toolkit}).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    (\emph{Composition})
    \label{lem:compose}
    If $c : \Sigma \rightarrow \Gamma^{\ell_c}$ and $d : \Gamma \rightarrow \Sigma^{\ell_d}$ are good, then $d \circ c$ is good: $\{d(w) : w \in D_c\}$ is comma-free over $\Sigma$, of uniform length $\ell_c\ell_d$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    An occurrence of $d(w)$ inside a sequence of $d$-images of $D_c$-codewords begins with $d(w[0]) \in D_d$, which by Self-Synchronization over $D_d$ starts on the $D_d$-grid; the whole block $d(w)$ then covers $D_d$-codewords spelling $d(w[0]) \cdots d(w[\ell_c{-}1])$, so by injectivity of $d$ the word $w$ occurs among the pre-image codewords, and if the block did not start on the outer ($\ell_c\ell_d$-) grid, $w$ would occur in a product of two $D_c$-codewords at a junction-straddling position -- against the comma-freeness of $D_c$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    (\emph{Retraction})
    \label{lem:retract}
    Let $h = d \circ c : \Sigma \rightarrow \Sigma^{\ell_c\ell_d}$ with $c, d$ good. Then $h$ is good, and both $h$ and a decoder $H_h$ with $H_h \circ h = \mathrm{id}$ are \emph{total} $L$-reachable: $h$ is $\mathrm{rep}_{|\Sigma|}$ with the single characters as patterns and the $h$-codewords as replacements, $H_h$ the same with the roles exchanged -- $\mathrm{rep}_n$ places no restriction on its patterns (Theorem~\ref{lem:multiple substitution}).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Comma-freeness is Lemma~\ref{lem:compose}; on an $h$-image the codeword occurrences are aligned (Self-Synchronization), so the freezing semantics of $\mathrm{rep}_n$ tiles the text exactly, replacing each $h(\sigma)$ by $\sigma$. Both $\mathrm{rep}_n$ have constant, nonempty patterns, hence are total.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    (\emph{Transfer})
    \label{thm:transfer}
    For all finite $\Sigma, \Gamma$ with $|\Sigma|, |\Gamma| \geq 2$, every good coding $c$, and every partial function $f$ on $\Sigma^*$:
    \begin{align}
        f \in L_\Sigma \;\iff\; f^c \in L_\Gamma,
    \end{align}
    and when $f$ is total the $\Gamma$-witness of the forward direction may be taken total (Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer-forward}). The $\Sigma$-witness of the backward direction is
    \begin{align}
        H_h\big(E'_d(h(X_1), \dots, h(X_n))\big),
    \end{align}
    where $E'$ is the given $\Gamma$-witness of $f^c$, $E'_d = (E')^d$, $d$ is any good coding $\Gamma \rightarrow \Sigma^{\ell_d}$, and $h = d \circ c$; its node count is $|E'|$ plus a constant depending only on the two alphabets.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Direction 1 is Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer-forward}. For Direction 2 let $E'$ over $\Gamma$ witness $f^c$. Every $h(X_i) = d(c(X_i))$ lies in $\mathrm{image}(d)$, where $E'_d$ computes $d$ of the $E'$-value (Direction 1, for the coding $d$); so
    \begin{align*}
        \llbracket E'_d \rrbracket\big(h(\vec X)\big)
        \;=\; d\big(\llbracket E' \rrbracket(c(\vec X))\big)
        \;=\; d\big(c(f(\vec X))\big) \;=\; h\big(f(\vec X)\big),
    \end{align*}
    and $H_h$ returns $f(\vec X)$ by the Retraction lemma. Definedness tracks $f$'s: $h$ and $H_h$ are total, and $E'_d$ is defined at the $h$-images exactly when $E'$ is at the $c(\vec X)$ -- strictness carries $E'$'s undefinedness through the total wrappers unchanged. The node-count claim follows from Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer-forward} and the constant patterns of the two $\mathrm{rep}_n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    (\emph{Alphabet-Uniform Questions})
    \label{cor:uniform}
    The right pass $\mathrm{r}$ (the collapse criterion of Theorem~\ref{thm:union-collapse}), the once node $[\,X_1/X_2\,]_1 X_3$ read as a three-argument function, and every $k$-th-occurrence node are \emph{coding-equivariant} -- each commutes with every good coding, by Lemma~\ref{lem:pass-transfer} -- so each of these reachability questions is a single question, not one per alphabet: the answer over one alphabet with at least two letters is the answer over every. The same holds for $\mathrm{rev}$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:rev-uniform} below. Consequently any negative answer over one alphabet excludes the conjugate over \emph{every} alphabet of at least two letters, and Conjecture~\ref{conj:union}, if true over one alphabet, is true over every.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    Equivariance plus Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer}: if $F$ commutes with the good coding $e$, then a $\Sigma$-witness for $F$ conjugates along $c$ to a $\Gamma$-function which, composed with the retraction $q = c \circ e$, computes the native $F$ over $\Gamma$ -- the identical construction as in Theorem~\ref{thm:rev-uniform}, with the equivariance of Lemma~\ref{lem:pass-transfer} in place of the palindrome clause. Concretely, for $e : \Gamma \rightarrow \Sigma^k$ good and $q = c \circ e$:
    \begin{align*}
        \llbracket E^c \rrbracket\big(q(\vec T)\big) \;=\; c\big(\llbracket E \rrbracket(e(\vec T))\big) \;=\; c\big(F(e(\vec T))\big) \;=\; c\big(e(F(\vec T))\big) \;=\; q\big(F(\vec T)\big),
    \end{align*}
    and $H_q$ returns $F(\vec T)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Reversal}

Reversal is the one equivariance that codings do not enjoy for free: a letter-by-letter reversal reverses \emph{each codeword individually}, so $c(\mathrm{rev}\,S) = \mathrm{rev}(c(S))$ holds iff every codeword is a palindrome. The naive ``rev on the code image'' is not the reversal of the image; the repair is to retract along a coding whose outer half is palindromic.

\begin{lemma}
    (\emph{Palindromic Dictionaries})
    \label{lem:palindrome}
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item $c(\mathrm{rev}\,S) = \mathrm{rev}(c(S))$ for all $S$ iff every codeword of $c$ is a palindrome; then $e(\mathrm{rev}\,T) = \mathrm{rev}(e(T))$ for every such $e$.
        \item (\emph{Middle-marker family.}) For $\mu \in \Sigma$ and $m \geq 1$, $\{\, \alpha\,\mu\,\mathrm{rev}(\alpha) : \alpha \in (\Sigma \setminus \mu)^m \,\}$ is comma-free and palindromic, of size $(|\Sigma|-1)^m$ -- unbounded for $|\Sigma| \geq 3$.
        \item Over the binary alphabet the family degenerates (size 1), but size-3 palindromic comma-free dictionaries exist: $\{\mathtt{aabaa}, \mathtt{ababa}, \mathtt{abbba}\}$ and $\{\mathtt{aabaa}, \mathtt{babab}, \mathtt{bbabb}\}$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    (i) is immediate on one-letter words. (ii) $\mu$ occurs in each codeword only at the exact middle, and in $u \cdot v$ only at the two middles; an occurrence of a codeword has its $\mu$ at $p + m$, hence $p \in \{0, 2m+1\}$ -- aligned. (iii) by inspection.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    (\emph{Reversal Uniformity})
    \label{thm:rev-uniform}
    $\mathrm{rev} \in L$ over one alphabet with at least two letters iff over every: given a witness $E$ over $\Sigma$, a good $c : \Sigma \rightarrow \Gamma^\ell$, and a good \emph{palindromic} $e : \Gamma \rightarrow \Sigma^k$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:palindrome}), $q \triangleq c \circ e$ gives
    \begin{align}
        \mathrm{rev}_\Gamma \;=\; H_q \circ \llbracket E^c \rrbracket \circ q .
    \end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    $q(T) = c(e(T))$, so $\llbracket E^c \rrbracket(q(T)) = c(\mathrm{rev}\, e(T))$ by Direction 1; the palindrome clause gives $\mathrm{rev}\, e(T) = e(\mathrm{rev}\, T)$, hence $\llbracket E^c \rrbracket(q(T)) = c(e(\mathrm{rev}\,T)) = q(\mathrm{rev}\,T)$, and $H_q$ returns $\mathrm{rev}\,T$. The needed dictionaries exist for every pair of alphabets: from a source of at least three letters use the middle-marker family for $e$; from a binary source, first move to a ternary alphabet along the size-3 binary dictionary of Lemma~\ref{lem:palindrome}(iii) -- a coding of three letters needs three codewords, and three exist -- and then proceed as before; $c$ itself is never required to be palindromic (Lemma~\ref{lem:dict} suffices), and $D_q = \{c(e(\gamma))\}$ is comma-free by Lemma~\ref{lem:compose}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    (\emph{No Contradiction with the Native Results})
    \label{rem:native}
    Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer} conjugates; Proposition~\ref{prop:del-leftmost} speaks of \emph{native} functions, and the two live in different conjugacy classes. The binary witness $W$ of the leftmost deletion transfers to a ternary alphabet as the \emph{block-level} function $[\,\epsilon/c(b)\,]_1$ on the image of $c$ -- it deletes an $\ell$-block, not a character, and is not the native ternary $[\,\epsilon/\gamma\,]_1$. Nor is the $\ell = 1$ coding a loop-hole: an injective character map is (trivially) good, and conjugating the binary once-deletion along it yields the native ternary function \emph{restricted to a two-letter sub-alphabet} -- reachable as a partial function, exactly the anchored-domain face of the phenomenon, and consistent with the resistance being about general position over three letters. Finally, the once-calculus -- and the rightmost calculus -- have transfer theorems of their own: Lemma~\ref{lem:pass-transfer} is scan-direction-agnostic, the first coded occurrence being the coding of the first native one.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The Reversal Frontier}
\label{ssec:frontier}

Theorem~\ref{thm:rev-uniform} makes $\mathrm{rev} \in L$ a single question per alphabet size; this subsection maps where its answer already lies, with both signs proved. Over unbounded alphabets it is negative at every depth, for a structural reason: on inputs whose letters are pairwise distinct and avoid the expression's constants, every expression of every shape and depth outputs \emph{constants interleaved with complete forward copies of the input} -- never a reversal (Theorem~\ref{thm:separable} and Corollary~\ref{cor:norev}); over a fixed alphabet, any witness's constants must cover all but one letter (Corollary~\ref{cor:witness-shape}). Over fixed alphabets the positive side is now complete at every \emph{fixed separator structure}: one separator and fixed middles of any shape (Theorem~\ref{thm:fixedmiddle}), and any number of separators with all runs varying -- the separator letters may repeat, so the same-letter family $\{\mathtt a^i\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^j\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^k\}$ where the separators collide is included (Theorem~\ref{thm:separators}) -- plus the rational symmetric correlations, which the general engine subsumes (Theorem~\ref{thm:correlations}). The same-letter engine also instantiates, at $h = 0$, the value equivalence that had been that family's reduction target (Proposition~\ref{prop:vh}). What remains is exactly \emph{varying separator count}: one expression computing reversal on all of $\Sigma^*$ at once -- and that question is now closed, negatively and unconditionally: no such expression exists, the failure effective at separator counts linear in the expression's pass count, in the dichotomy theorem with which the subsection closes; the structure lemma and the toll stated along the way (Proposition~\ref{prop:toll}) record what the run-level calculus can and cannot extract, as self-contained structural facts.

\paragraph{Separable rigidity.}

\begin{definition}
    (\emph{Separable})
    \label{def:separable}
    For an expression $E$ with constants from $\Gamma \subseteq \Sigma$, an input $w = x_0 x_1 \cdots x_{n-1} \in \Sigma^n$ is \emph{separable for $E$} if its letters are pairwise distinct and disjoint from $\Gamma$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    (\emph{Copy Form})
    \label{def:copyform}
    A value is in \emph{copy form} (with $M \geq 0$ copies) if it is $v_0\,w\,v_1\,w\,v_2 \cdots w\,v_M$, each middle block the whole word $w$ and each $v_j \in \Gamma^*$; for $M = 0$ the value is a constant word. The blocks $w$ are the \emph{instances}.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    (\emph{Copy Alignment})
    \label{lem:copyalign}
    Let $w$ be separable, $T = v_0 w v_1 \cdots w v_M$ and $X = u_0 w u_1 \cdots w u_{\pi}$ in copy form ($\pi \geq 0$, $X \neq \epsilon$). Then every occurrence of $X$ in $T$ either ($\pi = 0$) lies inside a single constant run $v_j$, or ($\pi \geq 1$) covers $\pi$ \emph{consecutive instances entirely} -- instance $a$ through $a + \pi - 1$ -- together with the $|u_0|$-letter suffix of $v_{a-1}$ and the $|u_\pi|$-letter prefix of $v_{a+\pi}$, the interior gaps matching exactly. No match window starts or ends strictly inside an instance.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    In a copy-form value the letters of $w$ occur only in the instances; a letter $x_j$ occurs once per instance, at offset $j$, and nowhere else (constant runs are $\Gamma$-words; an instance spells $w$, whose letters are pairwise distinct). In particular $x_0$ occurs exactly at instance starts, and any $n$ consecutive letters spelling $w$ are exactly one instance. If $\pi = 0$, $X$ is a $\Gamma$-word, and a window spelling it cannot meet an instance (a non-$\Gamma$ letter would have to equal a $\Gamma$ letter); it lies in a single constant run. If $\pi \geq 1$, let the window start at $s$. $X$'s first non-$\Gamma$ letter is $x_0$, at $X$-offset $|u_0|$, so $T[s + |u_0|] = x_0$ is an instance start $p_a$; $X$'s first block is $n$ contiguous letters spelling $w$, so $[p_a, p_a + n)$ is exactly instance $a$ and coincides with $X$'s first block. Inductively, if $X$'s $i$-th block coincides with instance $a + i - 1$, the next $|u_i|$ window letters must spell the $\Gamma$-word $u_i$: they cannot be fewer than the run $v_{a+i-1}$ leaves (the following letter of $X$ is $x_0$, non-$\Gamma$, but a shorter gap lands inside the $\Gamma$-run $v_{a+i-1}$), nor more (a longer gap reaches into instance $a+i$, whose letters are non-$\Gamma$, or past the text's end). So $u_i = v_{a+i-1}$ and $X$'s $(i{+}1)$-st block starts at instance $a+i$'s start, coinciding with it. The trailing gap $u_\pi$ is a prefix of $v_{a+\pi}$, the leading gap $u_0$ a suffix of $v_{a-1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    (\emph{Preservation})
    \label{lem:preserve}
    With $T$, $X$ as in Lemma~\ref{lem:copyalign} and $y = z_0 w z_1 \cdots w z_\nu$ in copy form, the value $[\,y/X\,]T$ (greedy leftmost-first, never rescanning inserted text) is in copy form: its instances are the instances of $T$ not covered by any match, whole and in order, plus $\nu$ fresh copies of $y$'s instances per match site.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    The scan selects pairwise disjoint windows, leftmost first, resuming after each; inserted text is never re-entered, so every selected window is an occurrence of $X$ in untouched text. By Lemma~\ref{lem:copyalign} every selected window is a union of whole instances plus pieces of constant runs, so each instance of $T$ is covered by exactly one match or by none. The output interleaves the uncovered instances with the surviving constant pieces and, at each site, one copy of $y$ -- a copy-form value by hypothesis; the $\Gamma$-pieces and the blocks assemble into copy form.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    (\emph{Separable Rigidity})
    \label{thm:separable}
    For every expression $E$ (any shape, any depth) and every separable input $w$ with $|w| = n \geq 1$: whenever $\llbracket E \rrbracket(w)$ is defined it is in copy form,
    $\llbracket E \rrbracket(w) = v_0\,w\,v_1 \cdots w\,v_M$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Induction on the number of nodes of $E$. A constant is copy form with $M = 0$; the variable is the single instance ($M = 1$); a concatenation merges two copy forms at the junction ($v_M$ of the left and $u_0$ of the right concatenate into one $\Gamma$-word). For the pass node, the three children are smaller, so $T$, $X$ and $y$ are in copy form by the induction hypothesis; if $X = \epsilon$ the node is undefined (Definition~\ref{def:den}), and otherwise Lemma~\ref{lem:preserve} applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    (\emph{Reversal Fails on Separable Inputs})
    \label{cor:norev}
    For $n \geq 2$ and $w$ separable, $\llbracket E \rrbracket(w) \neq \mathrm{rev}(w)$: the output is either a $\Gamma$-word (while $\mathrm{rev}(w)$ contains every letter of $w$), or contains $w$ \emph{forwards} as a contiguous block, which $\mathrm{rev}(w)$ -- of the same length -- contains only if $w = \mathrm{rev}(w)$, impossible for pairwise distinct letters at $n \geq 2$. In the provenance reading (label each output character by its input position): the labels of a copy-form value are $M$ concatenated ascending runs $(0, \dots, n{-}1)$, so $E$ realizes no strictly decreasing provenance on separable inputs, at any depth.
\end{corollary}

\begin{theorem}
    (\emph{Unbounded Alphabets})
    \label{thm:rev-unbounded}
    For every expression $E$ and every $n \geq 2$ there are separable inputs of length $n$, and $E$ differs from reversal on every one of them: no expression computes reversal on all strings, and none computes it on $\Sigma^*$ for every finite $\Sigma$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Separable inputs of length $n$ exist whenever the alphabet offers $n$ letters outside $\Gamma$, and Corollary~\ref{cor:norev} excludes agreement on each.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    (\emph{Witness Shape})
    \label{cor:witness-shape}
    Over a fixed finite $\Sigma$, any $E$ computing $\mathrm{rev}$ on all of $\Sigma^*$ has $|\Sigma \setminus \Gamma| \leq 1$, where $\Gamma$ is the set of letters occurring in $E$'s constants: a witness's constants must cover all but one letter of the alphabet.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    If $|\Sigma \setminus \Gamma| \geq 2$, a two-letter separable input $xy$ with $x, y \in \Sigma \setminus \Gamma$ exists, and $E(xy) \neq \mathrm{rev}(xy) = \mathtt{yx}$ by Corollary~\ref{cor:norev}.
\end{proof}

The constraint $|\Sigma \setminus \Gamma| \leq 1$ is met with equality $0$ by the positive engines below -- they use every letter as a constant -- and is sharp in the sense that no obstruction can be read off the unused-letter count alone. The relation to the mirror question is direct: $\mathrm{rev} \in L$ over one alphabet of at least two letters iff over every (Theorem~\ref{thm:rev-uniform}), and the theorem above closes the unbounded end while leaving the fixed-alphabet end exactly at the frontier this subsection now maps.

\paragraph{The complement engine.}

All positive results below rest on one counting argument, used three ways. Fix a \emph{filler} letter $\mathtt a$ and call the others \emph{non-flank}.

\begin{lemma}
    (\emph{Self-Anchoring})
    \label{lem:anchor}
    Let $M$ be a nonempty word containing a non-flank letter, and let $s_1 < \dots < s_d$ be the offsets of its non-flank letters ($d \geq 1$). Then:
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item $M$ occurs exactly once in $\mathtt a^i M \mathtt a^k$, at offset $i$, for all $i, k \geq 0$;
        \item $\mathtt a^i M \mathtt a^k$ occurs exactly once in $\mathtt a^L M \mathtt a^L$, at offset $L - i$, for all $i, k, L \geq 0$ with $L \geq \max(i, k)$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    (i) If $M$ occurs at offset $p$, then for each $r$ the text letter at $p + s_r$ is $M[s_r] \neq \mathtt a$, a non-flank position of the text; the text's non-flank positions are exactly $i + s_1 < \dots < i + s_d$ (the flanking runs contribute none). So $\{p + s_r\} \subseteq \{i + s_r\}$, and both sets have $d$ elements, hence are equal; comparing increasing enumerations gives $p = i$. (ii) The pattern $\mathtt a^i M \mathtt a^k$ has the same $d$ non-flank letters, at pattern-offsets $i + s_r$; an occurrence at offset $p$ in $\mathtt a^L M \mathtt a^L$ forces $\{p + i + s_r\} = \{L + s_r\}$ by the same counting, hence $p = L - i$; the window then fits exactly when $L \geq i$ and $L \geq k$, both of which hold.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    (\emph{Fixed Middles})
    \label{thm:fixedmiddle}
    Let $M$ be any nonempty word containing a non-flank letter. Then
    \begin{align*}
        E_M \;\triangleq\; [\,\mathrm{rev}(M)/X\,]\,\big([\,\epsilon/M\,]X \;\cdot\; M \;\cdot\; [\,\epsilon/M\,]X\big)
    \end{align*}
    computes $\mathrm{rev}$ on $\{\, \mathtt a^{i} M \mathtt a^{k} : i, k \geq 0 \,\}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Let $w = \mathtt a^i M \mathtt a^k$ and $L = i + k$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:anchor}(i), $M$ occurs exactly once in $w$, at offset $i$, and the greedy leftmost scan deletes exactly that window (positions before it are all $\mathtt a$); the output $\mathtt a^{L}$ contains no $M$. The scrutinee is $\mathtt a^{L} M \mathtt a^{L}$, and the pattern is $X = w = \mathtt a^i M \mathtt a^k$, which by Lemma~\ref{lem:anchor}(ii) occurs exactly once in it, at offset $L - i$; the window consumes the last $i$ letters of the left run, $M$ exactly, and the first $k$ letters of the right run -- available since $L \geq i$ and $L \geq k$ -- so the match fires for all $i, k \geq 0$, with no boundary restriction. The pass replaces the window by $\mathrm{rev}(M)$ and never rescans, so the output is
    $\mathtt a^{L-i} \cdot \mathrm{rev}(M) \cdot \mathtt a^{L-k} = \mathtt a^{k}\,\mathrm{rev}(M)\,\mathtt a^{i} = \mathrm{rev}(w)$.
    For $M = \mathtt b$ this is the one-separator engine on $\{\mathtt a^i \mathtt b \mathtt a^j\}$: three pass nodes.
\end{proof}

The engine is a \emph{symmetrization plus complement}: the scrutinee rebuilds the input's content symmetrically around the fixed middle ($\mathtt a^L M \mathtt a^L$ carries no trace of the split $i + k = L$), and the pattern -- the input itself -- carves the complement out of it. Separator letters feed the same mechanism at any number of separators and at any multiplicity.

\begin{theorem}
    (\emph{Fixed Separator Structures})
    \label{thm:separators}
    Let $k \geq 1$, let $s_1, \dots, s_k$ be letters distinct from $\mathtt a$ -- not necessarily distinct from each other -- and let $W = \{\, \mathtt a^{r_0} s_1 \mathtt a^{r_1} s_2 \cdots s_k \mathtt a^{r_k} : r_0, \dots, r_k \geq 0 \,\}$. Then $\mathrm{rev}$ is computable on $W$, with pass depth $2k + O(1)$ and size $\Theta(k^2)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Write $S = r_0 + \cdots + r_k$ and let $\mathrm{mrg}$ be the chain of one-letter passes $[\,\epsilon/s\,]$ over the \emph{distinct} letters among $s_1, \dots, s_k$, applied to the input $X$: each deletes all copies of its letter, so $\mathrm{mrg} = \mathtt a^{S}$. The engine rests on two deletion primitives, one per direction. For a separator letter $s$ and a value $E$ whose runs are contiguous sums of the runs $r_0, \dots, r_k$ (hence each at most $S$) -- a property the construction below preserves at every stage -- set
    \begin{align*}
        \mathrm{del}_{\mathrm{last}}(E, s) \;&\triangleq\; [\,\epsilon/(s \cdot \mathrm{mrg} \cdot \mathtt a)\,]\,(E \cdot \mathrm{mrg} \cdot \mathtt a),\\
        \mathrm{del}_{\mathrm{first}}(E, s) \;&\triangleq\; [\,\epsilon/(\mathtt a \cdot \mathrm{mrg} \cdot s)\,]\,(\mathtt a \cdot \mathrm{mrg} \cdot E).
    \end{align*}
    In $E \cdot \mathrm{mrg} \cdot \mathtt a$ the last $s$ of $E$ is followed by the run $\rho + S + 1$ (its own following run $\rho \leq S$, then the merge, then the pad), while every other $s$-gap of $E$ is at most $S < S + 1$: the pattern $s \cdot \mathtt a^{S+1}$ fires only at the last $s$, and its window -- that $s$ and the next $S + 1$ letters -- consumes $E$'s $\rho$-letter tail and $S + 1 - \rho$ letters of the merge-and-pad, leaving $\rho$ letters in its place: the output is exactly $E$ with its last $s$ deleted, the natural glue kept (the deletion is absorbed by the merge). $\mathrm{del}_{\mathrm{first}}$ is the mirror, boosting the \emph{first} $s$'s preceding run. Repeated letters need no separate treatment: $m$ applications of $\mathrm{del}_{\mathrm{first}}$ at letter $s$ delete the $m$ leftmost copies of $s$, of $\mathrm{del}_{\mathrm{last}}$ the $n$ rightmost. Now set
    \begin{align*}
        L_m \;\triangleq\; \mathrm{del}_{\mathrm{first}}^{m-1}\,\mathrm{del}_{\mathrm{last}}^{k-m}\,X = \mathtt a^{r_0 + \cdots + r_{m-1}}\, s_m\, \mathtt a^{r_m + \cdots + r_k}
    \end{align*}
    (all separators but the $m$-th deleted, the runs merging around the survivor) and
    \begin{align*}
        D_m \;\triangleq\; [\,s_m/L_m\,]\,(\mathrm{mrg} \cdot s_m \cdot \mathrm{mrg}) = \mathtt a^{r_m + \cdots + r_k}\, s_m\, \mathtt a^{r_0 + \cdots + r_{m-1}},
    \end{align*}
    the complement engine of Theorem~\ref{thm:fixedmiddle} on the $m$-th projection: the pattern $L_m$ has the single non-flank letter $s_m$, at a unique position of the scrutinee $\mathtt a^S s_m \mathtt a^S$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:anchor}(ii) with $M = s_m$, $L = S \geq \max(r_0 + \cdots + r_{m-1},\, r_m + \cdots + r_k)$), so it fires once and outputs the complements $(S - (r_0 + \cdots + r_{m-1}),\, S - (r_m + \cdots + r_k))$ -- repeats in the separator word are irrelevant here, the scrutinee holding a single $s_m$. The concatenation
    \begin{align*}
        T' \;\triangleq\; D_k \cdot \mathtt a \cdot D_{k-1} \cdot \mathtt a \cdots \mathtt a \cdot D_1
    \end{align*}
    has the separators in reversed order, leading run $r_k$ and trailing run $r_0$ exact, and every inter-separator run equal to $(r_0 + \cdots + r_{m-1}) + 1 + (r_{m-1} + \cdots + r_k) = S + r_{m-1} + 1$. Finally one pass per distinct separator letter, $[\,s/(s \cdot \mathrm{mrg} \cdot \mathtt a)\,] = [\,s/s\,\mathtt a^{S+1}\,]$: its pattern fires at every $s$-position whose following run is at least $S + 1$ -- in $T'$, at every separator except the last (the rightmost, whose following run is the trailing $r_0 \leq S$) -- and each firing consumes the separator and exactly $S + 1$ letters of the following run, leaving $r_{m-1}$ before the next separator. The output is $\mathtt a^{r_k} s_k \mathtt a^{r_{k-1}} \cdots s_1 \mathtt a^{r_0} = \mathrm{rev}(X)$. All sub-expressions denote at the original input, so $\mathrm{mrg} = \mathtt a^S$ is stable throughout. The $\mathtt a$-pads and the $+1$ are paired edge-guards: without the $+1$ the last separator would fire whenever $r_0 = S$ (all other runs $0$) and eat the trailing run; the $k = 2$ instance below shows the pairing concretely. For $k = 1$ this is Theorem~\ref{thm:fixedmiddle} at $M = s_1$; when the letters are pairwise distinct, the same projection values $L_m$ are computed by plain chains of one-letter passes, which is the earlier distinct-separator engine -- the special case, with $4k^2 - 1$ pass nodes and pass depth $2k$ (tree counts), bounds the present engine supersedes ($k^2 + k + 1$ pass nodes in shared form) while admitting repeats.
\end{proof}

The $k = 2$ same-letter instance is the engine that closed the last fixed-structure family, and its mechanism is worth spelling out, because the deletion primitives do their work invisibly at the statement level. On $W_2 = \{\, \mathtt a^i \mathtt b\, \mathtt a^j \mathtt b\, \mathtt a^k : i, j, k \geq 0 \,\}$ with $S = i + j + k$ and $\mathrm{mrg} = \mathtt a^S$: the two projections are
\begin{align*}
    P_1 \;&=\; [\,\epsilon/(\mathtt b \cdot \mathrm{mrg} \cdot \mathtt a)\,]\,(X \cdot \mathrm{mrg} \cdot \mathtt a) \;=\; \mathtt a^i \mathtt b\, \mathtt a^{j+k},\\
    P_2 \;&=\; [\,\epsilon/(\mathtt a \cdot \mathrm{mrg} \cdot \mathtt b)\,]\,(\mathtt a \cdot \mathrm{mrg} \cdot X) \;=\; \mathtt a^{i+j} \mathtt b\, \mathtt a^k,
\end{align*}
merge-padded concatenations: the pattern $\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{S+1}$ fires only at the second separator, whose following run the merge boosts to $k + S + 1$ (the first separator's is $j \leq S < S + 1$), and the mirror only at the first. The boxes $C_c = [\,\mathtt b/P_2\,](\mathrm{mrg} \cdot \mathtt b \cdot \mathrm{mrg}) = \mathtt a^k \mathtt b\, \mathtt a^{i+j}$ and $B_b = [\,\mathtt b/P_1\,](\mathrm{mrg} \cdot \mathtt b \cdot \mathrm{mrg}) = \mathtt a^{j+k} \mathtt b\, \mathtt a^i$ then feed the concatenation $C_c \cdot \mathtt a \cdot B_b = \mathtt a^k \mathtt b\, \mathtt a^{S+1+j} \mathtt b\, \mathtt a^i$, and the final pass $[\,\mathtt b/(\mathtt a \cdot \mathrm{mrg} \cdot \mathtt b)\,]$ fires only at the second separator (the first's preceding run is $k \leq S < S + 1$; the middle run $S + 1 + j \geq S + 1$ always), shaving exactly $S + 1$ and leaving $j$: the output is $\mathrm{rev}$. The $\mathtt a$ pad between the boxes and the $+1$ in the final pattern are paired edge-guards (at $j = 0$ the middle run is exactly $S + 1$; at $i = j = 0$ the first separator's preceding run $k$ must stay below $S + 1$, which the $+1$ guarantees even when $k = S$). No middle-run isolation ever happens -- the middle is an oversupplied merge and the final pattern is merge-flavored -- which is how this family evaded every earlier strategy class: the wall was bypassed, not broken. Before the general engine, the same-letter family yielded this positive subset:

\begin{theorem}
    (\emph{Rational Symmetric Correlations})
    \label{thm:correlations}
    For all coprime $c \geq 0$, $d \geq 1$, $\mathrm{rev}$ is computable on
    $\{\, \mathtt a^i \mathtt b\, \mathtt a^{j} \mathtt b\, \mathtt a^k : d \cdot j = c \cdot (i + k) \,\}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    On the family, $j = c(i+k)/d$ is integral and $S = i + j + k = (c+d)t$ with $i + k = dt$; write $F = [\,\mathtt a^d/\mathtt a^{c+d}\,][\,\epsilon/\mathtt b\,]X$ and $M = [\,\mathtt a^c/\mathtt a^{c+d}\,][\,\epsilon/\mathtt b\,]X$: the inner pass deletes both separators giving $\mathtt a^{(c+d)t}$, and the outer pass tiles this exact multiple, so $F = \mathtt a^{dt} = \mathtt a^{i+k}$ and $M = \mathtt a^{ct} = \mathtt a^{j}$ exactly, with no leftover (a one-letter pass on a pure run divides it: $\mathtt a^{p q} \mapsto \mathtt a^{r q}$ for the pattern $\mathtt a^p$ and replacement $\mathtt a^r$). Then $E = [\,\mathtt b\,M\,\mathtt b/X\,]\,(F \cdot \mathtt b \cdot M \cdot \mathtt b \cdot F)$: the pattern $X$ has two separators with interior $\mathtt a^j$; by Lemma~\ref{lem:anchor}(ii)-type anchoring at the unique separator pair of the scrutinee $\mathtt a^{i+k} \mathtt b \mathtt a^{j} \mathtt b \mathtt a^{i+k}$, the interior matches exactly and the flanks dominate ($i + k \geq i$, $k$), so the pass fires once, leaving flank remnants $\mathtt a^{k}$ and $\mathtt a^{i}$ around the replacement $\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{j}\,\mathtt b$: the output is $\mathrm{rev}(X)$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The same-letter family: the value equivalence.}

\begin{proposition}
    (\emph{The $V_h$ Equivalence})
    \label{prop:vh}
    On $W_2 = \{\, \mathtt a^i \mathtt b\, \mathtt a^j \mathtt b\, \mathtt a^k : i, j, k \geq 0 \,\}$, $\mathrm{rev}$ is computable iff for some constant $h \geq 0$ the value
    \begin{align*}
        V_h \;=\; \mathtt a^{k}\,\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{j+h}\,\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{i+h}
    \end{align*}
    is computable on $W_2$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    $(\Leftarrow)$ $[\,\mathtt b/\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{h}\,]\,V_h$: the constant pattern $\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{h}$ fires at both separators (the following runs $j+h$, $i+h$ dominate $h$), replacing each by $\mathtt b$ and leaving $\mathrm{rev}$. $(\Rightarrow)$ $[\,\mathtt b \mathtt a^{h}/\mathtt b\,]\,E$ pads a reversal-computer to $V_h$. A whole slack class interpolates: every $\mathtt a^{k+d_1} \mathtt b \mathtt a^{j+c} \mathtt b \mathtt a^{i+d_2}$ with $d_1 + d_2 = h$ computes $\mathrm{rev}$ by one further constant pass ($\mathrm{STRIP}$ $[\,\mathtt a^{r_0-d_1} \mathtt b \mathtt a^{r_1-d_2}/\mathtt a^{r_0} \mathtt b \mathtt a^{r_1}\,]$, constants $r_0 \geq d_1$, $r_1 \geq d_2$); the class is closed under one-separator constant passes, with an affine action on $(d_1, c, d_2)$ independent of $i, j, k$.
\end{proof}

The equivalence is now witnessed at $h = 0$: $E_{\mathrm{rev}}$'s value \emph{is} $V_0$, and $V_1 = [\,\mathtt b\mathtt a/\mathtt b\,]\,E_{\mathrm{rev}}$ -- so the ``if'' direction is instantiated, and the same-letter family's question was answered by a construction, not by an obstruction. What the analysis retains is its instrument: $V_h$ remains invisible to every counting argument (its total content is $S + 2h$, an exact function of $S$), which is why the run-level structure lemma below is needed to see anything at all on this family. The final-pass analysis that located the target stands as the analysis of its class -- any two-separator final pass in one firing forces the replacement's middle run to be exactly $j$, with three suppliers (flank-family remnants, which reduce to the slack class above; merge-powers, which exist exactly on the rational symmetric correlations of Theorem~\ref{thm:correlations}; and the split $\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{j}\,\mathtt b$, which the toll below excludes) -- while the winning engine sits outside that class: a one-separator, merge-flavored pattern shaving an oversupplied middle and leaving $j$ as remnant. And the two one-separator projections $\mathtt a^i \mathtt b \mathtt a^{j+k}$ and $\mathtt a^{i+j} \mathtt b \mathtt a^k$, whose constructibility the analysis left as the open hinge, are exactly $E_{\mathrm{rev}}$'s first stage: the merge-padded scrutinees make the deletion consume the merge rather than a flank, which is why no toll ever applied to them.

\begin{lemma}
    (\emph{Run-Total Structure})
    \label{lem:structure}
    Fix $W_2$ and $S = i + j + k$. For every expression $E$ there is a locally finite partition of $[1,\infty)^3$ into cells -- finite on every bounded region -- obtained by, in order: slab levels pinning the tile counts of computed moduli over the value's boundedly many long runs; residue classes of $(i,j,k)$ modulo constants; and coincidence cuts by nonzero pinned polynomials; such that on each cell the $\mathtt a$- and $\mathtt b$-counts of $\llbracket E \rrbracket$'s value are functions of $S$ alone. \emph{Status:} proved unconditionally on the non-explosive stratum (no unbounded block repetition in any intermediate value); in general, reduced to a pinned-polynomial profile schema whose analytic core -- a template-sum lemma and a local-indistinguishability lemma, together forcing block multiplicities to enter only through the window counts of the passes that created them -- is proved, and whose residual case table (computed replacements at anchored sites, nested computed patterns on periodic regions, and explosive tilings -- b-bearing constant patterns on block interiors with computed replacements, the quadratic exhibit's class) is closed at \emph{proof-skeleton} level by a telescope lemma: every window count is an $S$-function on cells of a finitely parameterized arrangement, multiplicities enter only through template-group sums, and per-run counts telescope to letter totals, prior window counts, or pinned constants (the explosive triple nesting has the exact closed form $\mathtt a^{S + 2S^3}$). The schema's first degree-separation claim -- every run length affine in $(i,j,k)$ plus a pinned polynomial in $S$ alone, products arising only within the $S$-typed kind or against pinned constants -- was \emph{refuted as stated}: on the all-odd cell of the mod-$2$ refinement, $[\,\mathrm{mrg}/\mathtt{bb}\,][\,\mathtt{bb}/\mathtt{aa}\,]X$ outputs a singular run of length $S(i+j-2)/2 + 1$, the product of the $S$-function $S$ with the pinned polynomial $(i+j-2)/2$ (a merged run absorbing a \emph{partial} template-group sum), whose $k$-slope on a fixed-$S$ plane is $-S/2$ -- unbounded, hence not of that form. The repaired statement: run lengths live in the closure of \{affine junction parts; $S$-function template lengths\} under sums, products ($S$-function) $\times$ (pinned polynomial), and exact division -- affine $\times$ affine never arises -- and this bounds every slab index (exact division of the closure's polynomials by the computed moduli leaves quotient plus bounded jitter), so the partition is \emph{finite}, not merely locally finite, on the schema hypothesis: the same conclusion, carried by exact polynomial division plus a provenance recursion. That finiteness -- not mere local finiteness -- is load-bearing is the toll's own lesson: the $j$-levels form a countable $j$-pinning cover of every plane. This finite-partition form is what Proposition~\ref{prop:toll} invokes.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proposition}
    (\emph{The Middle-Run Toll})
    \label{prop:toll}
    Conditional on Lemma~\ref{lem:structure} in its finite-partition form: no expression computes $\mathtt a^{j}$ on $W_2$; likewise $\mathtt a^i$, $\mathtt a^k$, $\mathtt a^{i+k}$, $\mathtt a^{i+j}$, $\mathtt a^{j+k}$ -- and, by the counting argument's final paragraph, no affine target at all: no value $\mathtt a^{L}$ with $L$ affine in $(i,j,k)$ and not a function of $S$ alone. (Only the affine lengths are claimed: the partition's residue classes can in principle support residue-dependent lengths, which the diagonal count does not reach.)
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Suppose $E$ computes $\mathtt a^j$ on $W_2$. Invoke Lemma~\ref{lem:structure} in its finite-partition form -- finitely many cells in all; the status note derives this from the profile schema at its current skeleton level (the repaired degree statement plus the telescope), and on the non-explosive stratum the runs are affine and every slab index is bounded outright. Let $N$ be the number of cells and choose $S_0$ beyond every constant of $E$ with $S_0 > N + 2$. On the plane $\{S = S_0\}$ -- a triangle of $(S_0 - 1)(S_0 - 2)/2$ lattice points arranged in $S_0 - 2$ nonempty $j$-levels (the lines $j = c$, $c = 1, \dots, S_0 - 2$) -- a cell on which the $\mathtt a$-count is $h_r(S)$ meets a $j$-level $\{j = c\}$ only with $h_r(S_0) = c$: every output there has length $c$ and $\mathtt a$-count $h_r(S_0)$. So each cell meets at most one $j$-level, $N$ cells meet at most $N$ of the $S_0 - 2 > N$ levels, and some level is left uncovered -- but its points are covered, a contradiction. Hence some cell meets two $j$-levels: two points with the same $S_0$, the same cell, and different $j$, at which the $\mathtt a$-count is $h_r(S_0)$ both times while the outputs $\mathtt a^{j_1}$ and $\mathtt a^{j_2}$ have different lengths. Contradiction. The other five targets work via the corresponding coordinate's levels: $\mathtt a^i$ and $\mathtt a^{j+k}$ via the $i$-levels (on the plane, $j + k = S_0 - i$), $\mathtt a^k$ and $\mathtt a^{i+j}$ via the $k$-levels, $\mathtt a^{i+k}$ via the $j$-levels ($i + k = S_0 - j$). For an arbitrary target length $L$, affine in $(i,j,k)$ and not a function of $S$, the same count takes the general form: each cell's trace on the plane lies in the diagonal $\{L = h_r(S_0)\}$, a line meeting the triangle in at most $S_0 - 2$ points, so $N$ cells cover at most $N(S_0 - 2) < (S_0 - 1)(S_0 - 2)/2$ points once $S_0 \geq 2N + 3$ -- the level pigeonhole and the diagonal count are the same repair, the first phrased for the coordinate targets, the second for all affine ones. (The counting step replaces an earlier covering argument -- 'some two-dimensional cell's trace contains a segment' -- which is invalid on the integer lattice: finitely many thin lattice sets, the $j$-levels themselves, cover the triangle. Finiteness of the partition is load-bearing: the pieces $\{j = c\}$ form a countable $j$-pinning cover of every plane, so a merely locally finite partition does not suffice.) The toll is sharp in dimension: on one-parameter subfamilies it vanishes, and rightly -- on the palindrome slice $i = k = 1$, $[\,\epsilon/\mathtt{ab}\,]$ computes $\mathtt a^{j}$, $j$ being a function of $S = j + 2$ there.
\end{proof}

\noindent The toll is the fixed-alphabet companion of the unbounded-alphabet rigidity: over unbounded alphabets, no expression can even \emph{permute} separable inputs (Corollary~\ref{cor:norev}); over fixed alphabets, an expression on the colliding-separator family can permute -- reversal itself is computable there (Theorem~\ref{thm:separators}) -- but cannot \emph{extract}: every shortcut through the middle run is a value whose length betrays the split $i + j + k$, and the structure lemma prices it. The toll is thus a self-contained structural fact rather than a step toward an obstruction: the engine never needs the separator-free values it excludes -- every intermediate of the construction carries a separator or is a merge. That leaves the frontier in exactly one place. Every \emph{fixed} separator structure is computable, at any multiplicity and over any finite alphabet (Theorems~\ref{thm:fixedmiddle} and~\ref{thm:separators}); unbounded alphabets are closed negatively at every depth (Theorem~\ref{thm:rev-unbounded}); and between the two lies the one remaining question, \emph{varying separator count}: one expression computing reversal on all of $\Sigma^*$ at once. The engine's per-letter shaves are already count-adaptive -- each fires at every separator except the last, regardless of $k$; only the positional projections $L_m$ know $k$ -- and that question is now closed: no such expression exists, the failure effective at separator counts linear in the pass count (Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy}).

The architecture, in brief. A single sweep is a uniform interleave: one pass emits the \emph{same} replacement value at every firing, the survivors appearing in input order (the sweep normal form; three lines from the semantics, cross-checked against the evaluator). On inputs whose run values are pairwise distinct, this forces any pass that fires more than once down to replacements carrying at most one separator: disjoint copies of a multi-separator replacement would repeat an interior run profile that the distinctness of $\mathrm{rev}$'s runs forbids. That is the \emph{Final-Pass Lemma}, proved twice and independently -- from the construction side, on the super-increasing inputs $\mathtt a^{2^0}\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{2^1}\cdots\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{2^k}$ whose reversed runs are the distinct powers of two, and from the counting side, on any distinct-run input; the fixed-structure engine is its live instance ($343$/$343$ reversals with the top pass firing exactly once and replacement the single letter $\mathtt b$). (That family is base $2$ -- the boundary of super-increase, where the \emph{values} come cheap and only the order is priced; the no-go therefore stages on base $3$, and Proposition~\ref{dich:prop:boundary} records the boundary exactly.) So the reversal must already pre-exist in the scrutinee, as an in-order extraction with uniform tweaks, and a descent through the expression's DAG follows. The descent's two escape routes die: \emph{stratified picks} across many copies of a replacement die by value-range disjointness (the pieces kept from different copies sit at strictly decreasing output positions, forcing the replacement's own interior to carry the deep decreasing structure -- the descent recurses into it unchanged), and \emph{phase carving} dies by a tuning argument (each distinct deep run-size needs a flank tuned to that size, so carving $\sim k$ depths costs $\Omega(k)$ pass nodes -- a size lower bound consistent with the fixed-structure engine's $\Theta(k)$ depth and $\Theta(k^2)$ size); the demand-side theorems below price both kills exactly. A \emph{separation budget} then closes the merge-free regime outright: label each character by its input run and count \emph{reversed separations} (a separator with run $t{+}1$'s material immediately left of it, run $t$'s immediately right); reversal needs $k$ of them, while a merge-free derivation of $E$ supplies at most $4\,\#\mathrm{S}(E) + 2\,\#\mathrm{C}(E)$ however often its passes fire -- so any unifier must \emph{merge} input-run material into common runs on every input with more separators than its size. But merging is also the one resource that makes order-inversion cheap: a one-pass pollution witness achieves $k$ reversed separations from a fixed budget of $8$. The obstruction therefore lives in the mergey regime and in \emph{run-length exactness}, not in order alone.

\paragraph{The staging family.}
The no-go is staged on one family, and the family's boundary explains the choice. For a value $u$ over $\{\mathtt a, \mathtt b\}$, the \emph{gaps} of $u$ are the lengths of its maximal $\mathtt a$-blocks in order, zero entries allowed (one entry per $\mathtt b$ plus one); $u$ is \emph{distinct-run} when its gaps are positive and pairwise distinct. The \emph{staging family} is
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal D(k;3) \;\triangleq\; \mathtt a^{3^{0}}\,\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{3^{1}}\,\mathtt b\cdots \mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{3^{k}},
\end{equation*}
strongly super-increasing (each run exceeds the sum of all earlier ones), with $\mathtt a$-total $S = (3^{k+1}-1)/2$ and $k \ge 1$; more generally $\mathcal D(k;B)$ with runs $B^{0}, \ldots, B^{k}$ and $S = (B^{k+1}-1)/(B-1)$. Write $I(u,v) = \sum_{j=u}^{v} 3^{j} = (3^{v+1}-3^{u})/2$ for the interval sums, $\mathrm{BotSum}(t) = I(0,t)$ and $\mathrm{TopSum}(t) = I(t{+}1,k)$.

\paragraph{The mirror plant.}
For $w = \mathtt a^{i_0}\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{i_1}\mathtt b\cdots\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{i_k}$, reversal preserves the gap multiset and reverses the gap sequence: $\mathrm{rev}(w) = \mathtt a^{i_k}\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{i_{k-1}}\mathtt b\cdots\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{i_0}$. Equivalently, in the merge $\mathrm{mrg} = \mathtt a^{S}$ -- all of $w$'s $\mathtt a$-material fused -- the $m$-th separator sits at depth $i_m + \cdots + i_k$, the $\mathtt a$-material to its right, and $\mathrm{rev}(w)$ is obtained by planting each separator at the mirrored depth $i_0 + \cdots + i_{m-1}$. On $\mathcal D(k;3)$ the $k$ mirror targets are the depths $I(t{+}1,k) = \mathrm{TopSum}(t)$, $t = 0..k-1$, all at least $3^{k}$. Every reversal mechanism in the calculus is a way of buying these plants: the fixed-structure engine of this paper buys one per box, its hand-optimized $k = 2$ instance buys two, and the degenerate families fall to single purchases -- one cluster of plants is one block move, a periodic mirror is one phase shift. The rest of this subsection prices the plants at unbounded $k$.

\begin{proposition}[the base-2 degeneracy; why the staging family is base 3]
    \label{dich:prop:boundary}
    At $B = 2$ the family $\mathcal D(k;2)$ is the boundary of super-increase ($2^{j} = \sum_{i<j} 2^{i} + 1$) and \emph{telescopes}: with $\mathrm{mrg} = \mathtt a^{S}$,
    \begin{align*}
        E_{\mathrm{last}} \;&\triangleq\; [\,\epsilon/\mathtt b\,][\,\mathtt a/\mathtt{aa}\,]X \;=\; \mathtt a^{2^{k}},\\
        [\,\epsilon/\mathtt b\,][\,\mathtt{aa}/\mathtt a\,]X \;&=\; \mathtt a^{2^{k+2}-2},\\
        E_{\mathrm{cbox}} \;&\triangleq\; [\,\mathtt b/(E_{\mathrm{last}}\mathtt b)\,](\mathrm{mrg}\,\mathtt b\,\mathrm{mrg}) \;=\; \mathtt a^{2^{k}-1}\mathtt b\,\mathtt a^{S},\\
        E_{\mathrm{smm}} \;&\triangleq\; [\,\epsilon/(\mathtt b\,\mathrm{mrg})\,]E_{\mathrm{cbox}} \;=\; \mathtt a^{2^{k}-1},\\
        E_{\mathrm{h2}} \;&\triangleq\; [\,\mathtt a/\mathtt{aa}\,]E_{\mathrm{smm}} \;=\; \mathtt a^{2^{k-1}}.
    \end{align*}
    Here $E_{\mathrm{last}}$ is the last run -- $\mathtt b$-free, at pass depth $2$ -- and $E_{\mathrm{smm}}$ the $\mathtt b$-free sum-minus-max. So at base 2 single-run extraction and sum-minus-max are free at depth $2$, both $r_k$ and $S - r_k$ are reachable in $O(1)$ depth, and no extraction-impossibility argument is available on that family. The staging family therefore migrates to base 3, where the gap separation holds. What dies at base 2 is not the values but the order. Every \emph{value} on the telescoping family is cheap: paddings, products, one-pass scalings by $2^{k}$, the plant-depth ladder in two constant passes -- five tools (the stepdown, the jumpdown, the scaler, the divider, the ladder) -- and both $\pm S$ padding directions round-trip, so pad-then-strip is dead. But the \emph{order} is priced: reversal must address all $k{+}1$ run-offsets, and addressing offset $j$ costs $\Theta(\min(j, k{-}j))$ by every mechanism examined -- the offset-cost ceiling; the rotation route dies at $k = 3$. Uniform $\mathrm{rev}$ on the base-2 family itself remains open; the dichotomy's linear bound below survives the boundary intact. Nothing is lost by the migration: $\mathcal D(k;3)$ is a subfamily of $\{\mathtt a, \mathtt b\}^{*}$, so a no-go there is a no-go on the full language.
\end{proposition}

The statement:

\begin{theorem}
    (\emph{The Reversal Dichotomy})
    \label{thm:dichotomy}
    Fix a finite alphabet $\Sigma$ with $|\Sigma| \geq 2$.
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item For every fixed separator structure -- any fixed separator word, any length, repeats allowed -- some expression computes $\mathrm{rev}$ on that family exactly (Theorem~\ref{thm:separators}); composing with the double-then-halve laundering of each letter restores the content with every atom sourced from constants, so with empty provenance.
        \item No single expression computes $\mathrm{rev}$ on all of $\Sigma^*$ -- indeed none computes it on the a-run/separator strings with unboundedly many separators. The failure is effective: each $E$ fails on the strongly super-increasing inputs $\mathcal D(k;3)$ (any fixed base $B \ge 3$ works; base 2 is the degenerate boundary, Proposition~\ref{dich:prop:boundary}) beyond separator count $k > c_{E}\,\#\mathrm{S}(E) + c'_{E}$ -- linear in the pass count.
        \item Over unbounded alphabets, reversal is not computable at all, at any depth (Theorem~\ref{thm:rev-unbounded}).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Parts (i) and (iii) are Theorems~\ref{thm:separators} and~\ref{thm:rev-unbounded}. Part (ii) assembles the chain below. \emph{Order:} by Lemma~\ref{dich:lem:DECOMP} a unifier has a pass-topped sub-expression reversing inputs with unboundedly many separators; by the Final-Pass Lemma (Lemma~\ref{dich:lem:FP}) the reversed gap sequence pre-exists in its scrutinee up to one splice, and the descent through the DAG recurses, its two escape routes killed (Propositions~\ref{dich:escapeA} and~\ref{dich:escapeB}). \emph{Budget:} by Lemma~\ref{dich:lem:SB} any unifier must merge input-run material on every input with more separators than its size, and by the payment theorem (Lemma~\ref{dich:lem:PAYMENT}) every mergey flip surviving to the output is stripped by downstream exact deep cuts. \emph{Supply:} the truncation ledger (Theorem~\ref{thm:TL}) pins what a computed flank can be, and the supply lemmas cap the exact deep cuts at $c\,\#\mathrm{S}(E)$ with $E$-fixed $c$ -- at most four cut values per pass node (Lemma~\ref{dich:lem:L2.2}), at most two fully-consumed deep sizes per flank pattern (Lemma~\ref{dich:lem:flankcap}), exact cuts at top-offset $d$ requiring slope $3^{-d}$ (Lemma~\ref{dich:lem:L2.1});
